and adding in a row labelled
a_{1}
in the appropriate place.
For the filling of
\bar{D}
, from Theorem
•
‣
4.2
we know that we really only need to look at what happens the
\mathbb{L}
-shapes.
Applying Theorem
5.2
to an
\mathbb{L}
-shape gives:

\longleftarrow
could be
\,+\,
or
0
+
+
\cdots
+
+
\cdots\cdots
+

where now every
\,+\,
in the horizontal string of
\,+\,
’s turns into its own
\mathbb{L}
, just with no horizontal part.

5.3.
Proof of the algorithm

Finally, we show that the algorithm in Section
5.1
actually gives us the correct

L

-diagram.
We already have that
D
is a

L

-diagram of type
(k+1,n)
from Theorem
4
, so what’s left is to show that
D
is loopless, has the right dimension, and has
\pi
as its associated decorated permutation.

\bpoint

Dimension and looplessness of
D

Other than verifying that the algorithm is correct, using the visual perspective of the algorithm, we can clearly see how the dimension of
D
arises in relation to the dimension of
\hat{D}
.
Namely, it comes from having at least one
\,+\,
in every column of
D
, with the number of additional
\,+\,
’s being exactly the number of non-last
\,+\,
’s in
\hat{D}
.

\tpointn

Theorem

\statement

Under this filling,
D
is loopless and has dimension
\dim(S_{D})=\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-2k+(n-1)
.

Proof.

For this proof, we only need Corollary
•
‣
4.2
and refer to (i) and (ii) from that statement.

First, it follows automatically from having
\geq 1

\,+\,
in every column of
D
that
D
is loopless.
For the dimension
S_{D}
, (i) and the
+1
in (ii) gives one
\,+\,
in every column of
D
for a total of
n-(k+1)

\,+\,
’s.

Summing up what’s left over in (ii) gives:

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}s_{\ell}+t_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell}s_{\ell}+\sum_{\ell}t_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\left(\#\text{ of non-last $\,+\,$'s in $\hat{D}$ }-\#\text{ of non-last $\,+\,$'s in col. $a_{n}$ of $\hat{D}$ }\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\left(\#\text{ of non-last $\,+\,$'s in col. $a_{n}$ of $\hat{D}$ }\right)

\displaystyle=\#\text{ of non-last $\,+\,$'s in $\hat{D}$ }

\displaystyle=\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-k

where the sums run over columns
\ell\neq a_{n}
in
\hat{D}
with at least one
\,+\,
.
The equalities come from:

•

Summing over
s_{\ell}
gives the number of non-last
\,+\,
’s in each column in
\hat{D}
excluding column
a_{n}
.

•

Summing over
t_{\ell}
gives the number of rows of type (II) in
\hat{D}
with last
\,+\,
’s not in column
a_{n}
, which in other words is the number of non-last
\,+\,
’s in column
a_{n}
.

•

As
\hat{D}
is co-loopless and there are
k
rows all with at least one
\,+\,
, there are
k
last
\,+\,
’s out of a total of
\dim(S_{\hat{D}})
, which gives us the last equality.

Putting the two together gives:

\displaystyle\#\text{ of $\,+\,^{\prime}s$ in $D$}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\text{cols in (i)}}1+\sum_{\text{cols in (ii)}}s_{\ell}+t_{\ell}+1

\displaystyle=n-(k+1)+\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-k

\displaystyle=\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-2k+(n-1)

which verifies that
\dim(S_{D})=\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-2k+(n-1)
as needed.
∎

\bpoint

D
corresponds to the correct decorated permutation

Recall that
\hat{D}
is a co-loopless

L

-diagram of type
(k,n)
associated to the co-loopless decorated permutation
\hat{\pi}
with
k

\hat{\pi}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&2&\cdots&n\\

Applying the algorithm in Section
5.1
constructs a loopless

We finally prove that

\statement

\begin{cases}\pi(i)=\hat{\pi}(i+1)=\begin{cases}a_{i}\neq i+1&\text{ for non-fixed points $i+1$ of $\hat{\pi}$ }\\
i+1&\text{ for fixed points $i+1$ of $\hat{\pi}$ }\end{cases}&\text{ for }i\neq n\\
\pi(n)=\hat{\pi}(1)=a_{n}&\end{cases}

Recall that to get from
D
to its decorated permutation, we go through its pipe dream (see Section

).
Starting from the label

on the NW border of

To read off the pipes from

Then boxes with

The pipes turn leftwards if it was originally going up, or upwards if originally going to the left.

b_{T}=a_{n}
with a
\,+\,
at
(a_{n},j)
.
Column
\ell_{m}=\ell_{s-1}
stays the same as before with
\,+\,
’s at
(a_{n},\ell_{m})
and
(b_{m},\ell_{m})
, and 0’s in-between, in
D
:

\hat{D}
j=\ell_{m+1}
a_{n}
+
+
+
\ell_{m}
b_{m-1}
b_{m}
\longmapsto
{D}
j=\ell_{m+1}
+
+
+
\ell_{m}
b_{m}
a_{n}

In the special case of
a_{n}>b_{1}
where
i+1=\ell_{1}
, now the
\mathbb{L}
in column
\ell_{1}=\ell_{s}
in
D
will have a
\,+\,
at
(a_{n},\ell_{1})
, since
\ell_{1}>a_{n}
and thus its
b_{B}=a_{n}
.
There is also still a
\,+\,
at
(b_{1},\ell_{1})
, since
b_{1}<a_{n}
and thus is of type (II), which aligns with the
\ell_{s}
as in the general case.

\hat{D}
i+1=\ell_{1}
a_{n}
+
+
+
\ell_{2}
b_{1}
b_{2}
\longmapsto
{D}
i+1=\ell_{1}
a_{n}
+
+
+
+
\ell_{2}
b_{1}
b_{2}

The analysis on
i+1
and
i
stays exactly the same as before, with the slight exception of
a_{n}>b_{1}
.
Since we have
i\geq a_{n}
with a
\,+\,
at
(a_{n},\ell_{1})
in
D
, now
a_{n}
plays the role of
b_{1}
and we use the same arguments as before, even if
i=a_{n}
in which case we consider
i
to be a row.
In all cases, we get that
\pi(i)=j
as needed for paths in (iv).

Putting (i)–(iv) together, we are now done since for all paths
\hat{\pi}(i+1)=j
we get
\pi(i)=\hat{\pi}(i+1)=j
!
∎

\bpoint

T-duality on the level of

L

-diagrams

With the proof that
D
gives the correct decorated permutation complete, we finally conclude by putting everything together.
Theorems
4
,
5.3
and
5.3
, tell us that in fact, the algorithm given in Section
5.1
is telling us what the T-duality map (see Section
4.3
) looks like on the level of

L

-diagrams.

\tpointn

Theorem

\statement

Given a co-loopless

L

-diagram
\hat{D}
of type
(k,n)
, the algorithm given in Section
5.1
constructs a loopless

L

-diagram
D
of type
(k+1,n)
such that

•

the dimensions of the positroid cells indexed by
D
and
\hat{D}
relate via

\dim(S_{D})=\dim(S_{\hat{D}})-2k+(n-1),

•

and the associated decorated permutations to
D
and
\hat{D}
relate via T-duality.

That is, the algorithm gives the T-duality map from
\hat{\pi}\mapsto\pi
on the level of

L

-diagrams.

\newrefsegment

Part III
Final thoughts

Chapter 6
Conclusion

6.1.
Further directions

In Part
I
, we started with the tale of the
c_{2}
-invariant as an avatar for the Feynman period and saw how combinatorial techniques involving enumerating edge bipartitions through swapping arguments were able to resolve the
p=2
case of the long sought after completion conjecture for the
c_{2}
-invariant.
However, the story is not over as of course what we would really like is a proof of the full conjecture.

Thus, the pivotal next direction to look at is:

Using this idea of swapping edges around a vertex, or vertices, across the edge bipartition, can we generalize the counting arguments seen in Chapter
3
for higher primes
p
?

For
p>2
, the combinatorial interpretation of the
c_{2}
-invariant becomes much more intricate as now we are enumerating the ways of distributing
p-1
copies of each edge across
2p-2
polynomials, where
p-1
of them come from spanning trees and the other
p-1
arises from particular spanning 2-forests.
Comparing to the
p=2
case, recall that we were bipartitioning one copy of each edge into a spanning tree, spanning 2-forest pair.
The difficulty in higher
p
is that now there no longer needs to be a nice pairing up of trees and forests that exactly partition full sets of edges from the graph.
Optimistically, Yeats
[
Yea18f
]
has been able to first answer this question in the
T
-case where we only needed to swap around 2-valent vertices.
The obstacle, naturally, in the
S
and
R
cases is in how to generalize this to include control vertices or perhaps instead how to circumvent the need for control vertices.

Finally, one could also ask about how much farther these enumerative techniques can take us.
Can they answer other questions about the
c_{2}
-invariant or compute the
c_{2}
’s for new families of graphs?

In Part
II
, we looked at the tale of the combinatorial T-duality map as a bridge between triangulations of the hypersimplex and triangulations of the amplituhedron and what this map looked like on

L

-diagrams.
One result, other than the nice structure of the

L

-diagrams arising from T-duality, was that we could directly see where the dimensional relationship between the positroid cells on either side of the map manifests from.

Continuing with this thread, a natural general question to further investigate is what other properties of positroids or triangulations can be determined through looking at T-duality via

L

-diagrams?
Two specific directions that could be of interest are:

•

The T-duality map turns out to be a special case of the
\rho_{A}(\pi)
map defined by Benedetti, Chavez and Tamayo
[
BCTJ22c
]
when characterizing quotients of uniform positroids.
Thus, can we extend our algorithm from Chapter
5
to this more general cyclic shift map?

•

In
[
PSBW21c
]
, Parisi, Sherman-Bennett, and Williams, were able to prove Theorem
4.3
through looking at the T-duality map via plabic graphs.
Thus, are there any interesting connections between the two perspectives on T-duality outside of the canonical bijection between

L

-diagrams and plabic graphs from
[
Pos06c
]
? Furthermore, is there any structure to the

L

-diagrams underlying the triangulations on either side of T-duality?

6.2.
Final thoughts

Through the underlying theme of emerging combinatorics in the world of scattering amplitudes, one could see the parallels between the stories of the
c_{2}
-invariant and the T-duality map.
Both branched off of the need to better understand the scattering amplitude, one through Feynman diagrams in scalar
\phi^{4}
-theory and one through on-shell diagrams in
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM.
Both then looked at related but simpler, and in some sense nicer, objects with interesting connections to many areas of mathematics, one reducing to the
c_{2}
-invariant and the other reducing to positroid cells of the positive Grassmannian.
In the end, both then converged to the idea that looking at these objects through a combinatorial perspective can be a powerful tool in answering questions about them, one being the completion conjecture for the
c_{2}
-invariant and the other being the relationship between triangulations of the amplituhedron and the hypersimplex.
At the heart of it all, they showcased a wonderful tale of beautiful mathematics at the intersection of combinatorics and quantum field theory!
